========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… it’s a bit embarrassing to say out loud, but I’m here because I’ve found myself organizing my whole house, really, around avoiding spiders. Not just, you know, “oh I don’t like them,” but actually changing what I do.

For example, I keep putting off doing laundry because the machines are in the basement, and I start thinking there might be spiders down there, especially in the corners or near the pipes. I avoid going into the garden shed even when I need something simple. And I’ve even paid people to do small jobs around the house that I absolutely know how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I might see one.

It feels ridiculous because I’m a grown woman, I teach teenagers for a living, I manage plenty of things perfectly well… but with this, I just end up arranging everything so I don’t have to face it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… very anxious, actually. Even just thinking about one can set me off. I know, logically, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I can say that sitting here, and I do understand it.

But the moment I picture one—like dropping out of a cardboard box, or sitting near the washing machine in the basement—it’s as if it becomes enormous in my mind. Not necessarily physically enormous, but threatening, like I’m suddenly on high alert. And then I feel embarrassed, because part of me is thinking, “Come on, it’s a spider,” but my body reacts as if it’s something much more dangerous. Even a picture can make me tense up if I’m not expecting it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s really spiders. I mean, I don’t love every insect, of course, and I might swat a fly like anyone else, but this particular reaction—the sort of… full-body alarm feeling—is tied to spiders.

With a spider, even if I’m just imagining it, I can feel myself tense up. Like I know perfectly well that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me, and I can say that quite calmly right now. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer in the basement, it suddenly feels huge and threatening in my head. Not huge like it’s actually enormous, but like it takes up all the space in the room somehow. And then I feel silly and embarrassed because I know my reaction doesn’t match the actual danger.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.

And then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.

So yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.

I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.

With spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.

It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other animals or things that resemble spiders, like insects with many legs?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.\n\nWith spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.

With spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. But then the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, it feels enormous and threatening in my mind. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.

And I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.

Like, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.

And I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.

I avoid going into the garden shed even when I need something simple. Not in the sense of, like, checking the stove ten times or locking and unlocking the door or anything like that. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.

And I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.

I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.

It tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby. But an object or something that only sort of resembles one — I can’t think of a clear example where that’s set me off in the same way.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it’s a bit embarrassing to say out loud, but I’m here because I’ve found myself organizing my whole house, really, around avoiding spiders. Not just, you know, “oh I don’t like them,” but actually changing what I do.\n\nFor example, I keep putting off doing laundry because the machines are in the basement, and I start thinking there might be spiders down there, especially in the corners or near the pipes. I avoid going into the garden shed even when I need something simple. And I’ve even paid people to do small jobs around the house that I absolutely know how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nIt feels ridiculous because I’m a grown woman, I teach teenagers for a living, I manage plenty of things perfectly well… but with this, I just end up arranging everything so I don’t have to face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking something multiple times?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of, like, checking the stove ten times or locking and unlocking the door or anything like that. I mean, I can be careful about things, and I’ll sometimes look around a room if I’m worried, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated routine that I feel I have to do over and over.\n\nIt’s more that I try not to get into the situation in the first place, if that makes sense. But repetitive checking… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about spiders or see a picture of one?\n\nPatient: Yes… very anxious, actually. Even just thinking about one can set me off. I know, logically, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I can say that sitting here, and I do understand it.\n\nBut the moment I picture one—like dropping out of a cardboard box, or sitting near the washing machine in the basement—it’s as if it becomes enormous in my mind. Not necessarily physically enormous, but threatening, like I’m suddenly on high alert. And then I feel embarrassed, because part of me is thinking, “Come on, it’s a spider,” but my body reacts as if it’s something much more dangerous. Even a picture can make me tense up if I’m not expecting it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety limited to spiders, or do you also feel anxious around other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders. I mean, I don’t love every insect, of course, and I might swat a fly like anyone else, but this particular reaction—the sort of… full-body alarm feeling—is tied to spiders.\n\nWith a spider, even if I’m just imagining it, I can feel myself tense up. Like I know perfectly well that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me, and I can say that quite calmly right now. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer in the basement, it suddenly feels huge and threatening in my head. Not huge like it’s actually enormous, but like it takes up all the space in the room somehow. And then I feel silly and embarrassed because I know my reaction doesn’t match the actual danger.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding spiders and the places where you might see them significantly interfered with your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other animals or things that resemble spiders, like insects with many legs?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.\n\nWith spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. But then the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, it feels enormous and threatening in my mind. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders ever triggered by something that reminds you of a spider, but isn't actually a spider?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.\n\nIt tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It isn’t only the actual spider in front of me, no. Seeing one is the worst, obviously, but it can start before that, just from the situation or the possibility of one being there.

Like I’ve said, I can be perfectly sensible in my head and know that a spider up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I’m not confused about that. But if I’m opening an old box and suddenly picture one dropping out, or if I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to reach, then it changes completely. In my mind it feels enormous, not necessarily in size but in threat, if that makes sense. It’s as if the whole room becomes about that spider and whether it might move or come near me.

I do feel embarrassed saying it out loud, because I know it sounds much bigger than it should be. But yes, it can be triggered by the thought of an actual spider being there, not just by seeing one.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.231\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it’s a bit embarrassing to say out loud, but I’m here because I’ve found myself organizing my whole house, really, around avoiding spiders. Not just, you know, “oh I don’t like them,” but actually changing what I do.\n\nFor example, I keep putting off doing laundry because the machines are in the basement, and I start thinking there might be spiders down there, especially in the corners or near the pipes. I avoid going into the garden shed even when I need something simple. And I’ve even paid people to do small jobs around the house that I absolutely know how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nIt feels ridiculous because I’m a grown woman, I teach teenagers for a living, I manage plenty of things perfectly well… but with this, I just end up arranging everything so I don’t have to face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking something multiple times?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of, like, checking the stove ten times or locking and unlocking the door or anything like that. I mean, I can be careful about things, and I’ll sometimes look around a room if I’m worried, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated routine that I feel I have to do over and over.\n\nIt’s more that I try not to get into the situation in the first place, if that makes sense. But repetitive checking… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about spiders or see a picture of one?\n\nPatient: Yes… very anxious, actually. Even just thinking about one can set me off. I know, logically, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I can say that sitting here, and I do understand it.\n\nBut the moment I picture one—like dropping out of a cardboard box, or sitting near the washing machine in the basement—it’s as if it becomes enormous in my mind. Not necessarily physically enormous, but threatening, like I’m suddenly on high alert. And then I feel embarrassed, because part of me is thinking, “Come on, it’s a spider,” but my body reacts as if it’s something much more dangerous. Even a picture can make me tense up if I’m not expecting it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety limited to spiders, or do you also feel anxious around other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders. I mean, I don’t love every insect, of course, and I might swat a fly like anyone else, but this particular reaction—the sort of… full-body alarm feeling—is tied to spiders.\n\nWith a spider, even if I’m just imagining it, I can feel myself tense up. Like I know perfectly well that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me, and I can say that quite calmly right now. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer in the basement, it suddenly feels huge and threatening in my head. Not huge like it’s actually enormous, but like it takes up all the space in the room somehow. And then I feel silly and embarrassed because I know my reaction doesn’t match the actual danger.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding spiders and the places where you might see them significantly interfered with your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other animals or things that resemble spiders, like insects with many legs?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.\n\nWith spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. But then the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, it feels enormous and threatening in my mind. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).\n  manifestation: I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box or sitting near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m embarrassed to admit.\n- key: S063\n  name: Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response\n  description: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.\n  manifestation: If I actually spot one, my chest tightens right away, my hands shake, and I either freeze in the doorway or back out so fast it’s like my body has decided before I can think.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: Owning this house has become more expensive and harder to manage because I’m paying for small maintenance help, letting chores back up, and avoiding parts of the property I need to use.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 1 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about thirteen months, since shortly after I moved into the house and found those spiders while unpacking.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S064\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.\n\nIt tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby. But an object or something that only sort of resembles one — I can’t think of a clear example where that’s set me off in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders only triggered by the actual presence or appearance of a spider, or can it also be triggered by other factors?\n\nPatient: It isn’t only the actual spider in front of me, no. Seeing one is the worst, obviously, but it can start before that, just from the situation or the possibility of one being there.\n\nLike I’ve said, I can be perfectly sensible in my head and know that a spider up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I’m not confused about that. But if I’m opening an old box and suddenly picture one dropping out, or if I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to reach, then it changes completely. In my mind it feels enormous, not necessarily in size but in threat, if that makes sense. It’s as if the whole room becomes about that spider and whether it might move or come near me.\n\nI do feel embarrassed saying it out loud, because I know it sounds much bigger than it should be. But yes, it can be triggered by the thought of an actual spider being there, not just by seeing one.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs it something about the way spiders look that triggers your fear, or is it more about what they are or what they might do?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s partly how they look, I suppose — the legs and the quick movement, that does get to me — but honestly I think it’s more about what I imagine they might do, or the fact that they’re there and unpredictable.

I can tell myself, quite clearly, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I’m opening a box and I picture one dropping out, or I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to put my hand, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not enormous like I think it’s literally huge, but enormous in my mind, like it takes over the whole situation.

And I’m embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s not as if I genuinely believe most spiders are dangerous. But in that moment, it feels like it might move toward me or appear suddenly, and that’s what really sets me off.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.231\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it’s a bit embarrassing to say out loud, but I’m here because I’ve found myself organizing my whole house, really, around avoiding spiders. Not just, you know, “oh I don’t like them,” but actually changing what I do.\n\nFor example, I keep putting off doing laundry because the machines are in the basement, and I start thinking there might be spiders down there, especially in the corners or near the pipes. I avoid going into the garden shed even when I need something simple. And I’ve even paid people to do small jobs around the house that I absolutely know how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nIt feels ridiculous because I’m a grown woman, I teach teenagers for a living, I manage plenty of things perfectly well… but with this, I just end up arranging everything so I don’t have to face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking something multiple times?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of, like, checking the stove ten times or locking and unlocking the door or anything like that. I mean, I can be careful about things, and I’ll sometimes look around a room if I’m worried, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated routine that I feel I have to do over and over.\n\nIt’s more that I try not to get into the situation in the first place, if that makes sense. But repetitive checking… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about spiders or see a picture of one?\n\nPatient: Yes… very anxious, actually. Even just thinking about one can set me off. I know, logically, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I can say that sitting here, and I do understand it.\n\nBut the moment I picture one—like dropping out of a cardboard box, or sitting near the washing machine in the basement—it’s as if it becomes enormous in my mind. Not necessarily physically enormous, but threatening, like I’m suddenly on high alert. And then I feel embarrassed, because part of me is thinking, “Come on, it’s a spider,” but my body reacts as if it’s something much more dangerous. Even a picture can make me tense up if I’m not expecting it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety limited to spiders, or do you also feel anxious around other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders. I mean, I don’t love every insect, of course, and I might swat a fly like anyone else, but this particular reaction—the sort of… full-body alarm feeling—is tied to spiders.\n\nWith a spider, even if I’m just imagining it, I can feel myself tense up. Like I know perfectly well that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me, and I can say that quite calmly right now. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer in the basement, it suddenly feels huge and threatening in my head. Not huge like it’s actually enormous, but like it takes up all the space in the room somehow. And then I feel silly and embarrassed because I know my reaction doesn’t match the actual danger.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding spiders and the places where you might see them significantly interfered with your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other animals or things that resemble spiders, like insects with many legs?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.\n\nWith spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. But then the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, it feels enormous and threatening in my mind. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders ever triggered by something that reminds you of a spider, but isn't actually a spider?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.\n\nIt tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby. But an object or something that only sort of resembles one — I can’t think of a clear example where that’s set me off in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders only triggered by the actual presence or appearance of a spider, or can it also be triggered by other factors?\n\nPatient: It isn’t only the actual spider in front of me, no. Seeing one is the worst, obviously, but it can start before that, just from the situation or the possibility of one being there.\n\nLike I’ve said, I can be perfectly sensible in my head and know that a spider up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I’m not confused about that. But if I’m opening an old box and suddenly picture one dropping out, or if I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to reach, then it changes completely. In my mind it feels enormous, not necessarily in size but in threat, if that makes sense. It’s as if the whole room becomes about that spider and whether it might move or come near me.\n\nI do feel embarrassed saying it out loud, because I know it sounds much bigger than it should be. But yes, it can be triggered by the thought of an actual spider being there, not just by seeing one.\n\nDoctor: Is it something about the way spiders look that triggers your fear, or is it more about what they are or what they might do?\n\nPatient: It’s partly how they look, I suppose — the legs and the quick movement, that does get to me — but honestly I think it’s more about what I imagine they might do, or the fact that they’re there and unpredictable.\n\nI can tell myself, quite clearly, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I’m opening a box and I picture one dropping out, or I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to put my hand, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not enormous like I think it’s literally huge, but enormous in my mind, like it takes over the whole situation.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s not as if I genuinely believe most spiders are dangerous. But in that moment, it feels like it might move toward me or appear suddenly, and that’s what really sets me off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders ever triggered by something that reminds you of a spider, but isn't actually a spider?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.\n\nIt tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby. But an object or something that only sort of resembles one — I can’t think of a clear example where that’s set me off in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders only triggered by the actual presence or appearance of a spider, or can it also be triggered by other factors?\n\nPatient: It isn’t only the actual spider in front of me, no. Seeing one is the worst, obviously, but it can start before that, just from the situation or the possibility of one being there.\n\nLike I’ve said, I can be perfectly sensible in my head and know that a spider up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I’m not confused about that. But if I’m opening an old box and suddenly picture one dropping out, or if I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to reach, then it changes completely. In my mind it feels enormous, not necessarily in size but in threat, if that makes sense. It’s as if the whole room becomes about that spider and whether it might move or come near me.\n\nI do feel embarrassed saying it out loud, because I know it sounds much bigger than it should be. But yes, it can be triggered by the thought of an actual spider being there, not just by seeing one.\n\nDoctor: Is it something about the way spiders look that triggers your fear, or is it more about what they are or what they might do?\n\nPatient: It’s partly how they look, I suppose — the legs and the quick movement, that does get to me — but honestly I think it’s more about what I imagine they might do, or the fact that they’re there and unpredictable.\n\nI can tell myself, quite clearly, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I’m opening a box and I picture one dropping out, or I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to put my hand, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not enormous like I think it’s literally huge, but enormous in my mind, like it takes over the whole situation.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s not as if I genuinely believe most spiders are dangerous. But in that moment, it feels like it might move toward me or appear suddenly, and that’s what really sets me off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s not just how they look, though the look of them doesn’t help — the legs, the way they can stay still and then suddenly move. But I think the bigger thing is what my mind does with them.

I know, rationally, that if there’s a spider in the corner of an old house, it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that perfectly calmly right now. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box I’m opening, or sitting near the washer where I have to reach in or put my hand near it, it suddenly feels… huge. Not physically huge, necessarily, but threatening, like it takes over everything in that moment.

And I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I do know it sounds out of proportion. It’s more the possibility of it suddenly being there, moving, coming near me — that’s what scares me more than just its appearance.

Like I know perfectly well that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me, and I can say that quite calmly right now. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders ever triggered by something that reminds you of a spider, but isn't actually a spider?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer in the basement, it suddenly feels huge and threatening in my head. Not huge like it’s actually enormous, but like it takes up all the space in the room somehow. And then I feel silly and embarrassed because I know my reaction doesn’t match the actual danger.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding spiders and the places where you might see them significantly interfered with your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other animals or things that resemble spiders, like insects with many legs?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.\n\nWith spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. But then the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, it feels enormous and threatening in my mind. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders ever triggered by something that reminds you of a spider, but isn't actually a spider?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.\n\nIt tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby. But an object or something that only sort of resembles one — I can’t think of a clear example where that’s set me off in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders only triggered by the actual presence or appearance of a spider, or can it also be triggered by other factors?\n\nPatient: It isn’t only the actual spider in front of me, no. Seeing one is the worst, obviously, but it can start before that, just from the situation or the possibility of one being there.\n\nLike I’ve said, I can be perfectly sensible in my head and know that a spider up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I’m not confused about that. But if I’m opening an old box and suddenly picture one dropping out, or if I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to reach, then it changes completely. In my mind it feels enormous, not necessarily in size but in threat, if that makes sense. It’s as if the whole room becomes about that spider and whether it might move or come near me.\n\nI do feel embarrassed saying it out loud, because I know it sounds much bigger than it should be. But yes, it can be triggered by the thought of an actual spider being there, not just by seeing one.\n\nDoctor: Is it something about the way spiders look that triggers your fear, or is it more about what they are or what they might do?\n\nPatient: It’s partly how they look, I suppose — the legs and the quick movement, that does get to me — but honestly I think it’s more about what I imagine they might do, or the fact that they’re there and unpredictable.\n\nI can tell myself, quite clearly, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I’m opening a box and I picture one dropping out, or I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to put my hand, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not enormous like I think it’s literally huge, but enormous in my mind, like it takes over the whole situation.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s not as if I genuinely believe most spiders are dangerous. But in that moment, it feels like it might move toward me or appear suddenly, and that’s what really sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders related to how they look or is it something else about them that scares you?\n\nPatient: It’s not just how they look, though the look of them doesn’t help — the legs, the way they can stay still and then suddenly move. But I think the bigger thing is what my mind does with them.\n\nI know, rationally, that if there’s a spider in the corner of an old house, it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that perfectly calmly right now. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box I’m opening, or sitting near the washer where I have to reach in or put my hand near it, it suddenly feels… huge. Not physically huge, necessarily, but threatening, like it takes over everything in that moment.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I do know it sounds out of proportion. It’s more the possibility of it suddenly being there, moving, coming near me — that’s what scares me more than just its appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues in any significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s affected them in a really significant way, no. Not in the sense of, you know, falling out with people or not being able to work with colleagues or anything like that.\n\nI think people close to me know I’m not good with spiders, and they might tease me a bit or be the one to deal with it if one appears, but I haven’t noticed it causing major problems in my relationships. At work I try not to make a big thing of it, because I’m aware it can sound a bit silly, especially as a teacher and as someone who’s meant to stay calm. But I can’t think of any serious impact on friends or family, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

But then if I’m opening a box and I picture one dropping out, or I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to put my hand, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not enormous like I think it’s literally huge, but enormous in my mind, like it takes over the whole situation.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s not as if I genuinely believe most spiders are dangerous. But in that moment, it feels like it might move toward me or appear suddenly, and that’s what really sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders related to how they look or is it something else about them that scares you?\n\nPatient: It’s not just how they look, though the look of them doesn’t help — the legs, the way they can stay still and then suddenly move. But I think the bigger thing is what my mind does with them.\n\nI know, rationally, that if there’s a spider in the corner of an old house, it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that perfectly calmly right now. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box I’m opening, or sitting near the washer where I have to reach in or put my hand near it, it suddenly feels… huge. Not physically huge, necessarily, but threatening, like it takes over everything in that moment.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I do know it sounds out of proportion. It’s more the possibility of it suddenly being there, moving, coming near me — that’s what scares me more than just its appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues in any significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s affected them in a really significant way, no. Not in the sense of, you know, falling out with people or not being able to work with colleagues or anything like that.\n\nI think people close to me know I’m not good with spiders, and they might tease me a bit or be the one to deal with it if one appears, but I haven’t noticed it causing major problems in my relationships. At work I try not to make a big thing of it, because I’m aware it can sound a bit silly, especially as a teacher and as someone who’s meant to stay calm. But I can’t think of any serious impact on friends or family, no.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs your fear of spiders more about the spider itself or about what might happen if you encounter one?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 54 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me, but the moment I picture one dropping out of a box or sitting near the washer, it feels enormous and threatening in a way I’m embarrassed to admit.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more about what might happen, I think. I mean, the spider itself — the way it looks — that bothers me, but it’s not only that.

I can look back and say, rationally, if there’s a spider sitting in the corner of an old house, it probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I do know that. But then if I imagine opening a box and one dropping out, or reaching near the washer and suddenly seeing one there where my hand has to go, it feels completely different. It feels enormous and threatening, even if I know it isn’t actually enormous.

It’s embarrassing, really, because I can hear how out of proportion it sounds. But in the moment it’s the suddenness, the not knowing what it’ll do, or it being too close to me — that’s what gets me.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F40.231\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… it’s a bit embarrassing to say out loud, but I’m here because I’ve found myself organizing my whole house, really, around avoiding spiders. Not just, you know, “oh I don’t like them,” but actually changing what I do.\n\nFor example, I keep putting off doing laundry because the machines are in the basement, and I start thinking there might be spiders down there, especially in the corners or near the pipes. I avoid going into the garden shed even when I need something simple. And I’ve even paid people to do small jobs around the house that I absolutely know how to do myself, just because I can’t make myself go into those spaces where I might see one.\n\nIt feels ridiculous because I’m a grown woman, I teach teenagers for a living, I manage plenty of things perfectly well… but with this, I just end up arranging everything so I don’t have to face it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over, like checking something multiple times?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. Not in the sense of, like, checking the stove ten times or locking and unlocking the door or anything like that. I mean, I can be careful about things, and I’ll sometimes look around a room if I’m worried, but I wouldn’t say I have a repeated routine that I feel I have to do over and over.\n\nIt’s more that I try not to get into the situation in the first place, if that makes sense. But repetitive checking… no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about spiders or see a picture of one?\n\nPatient: Yes… very anxious, actually. Even just thinking about one can set me off. I know, logically, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I can say that sitting here, and I do understand it.\n\nBut the moment I picture one—like dropping out of a cardboard box, or sitting near the washing machine in the basement—it’s as if it becomes enormous in my mind. Not necessarily physically enormous, but threatening, like I’m suddenly on high alert. And then I feel embarrassed, because part of me is thinking, “Come on, it’s a spider,” but my body reacts as if it’s something much more dangerous. Even a picture can make me tense up if I’m not expecting it.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety limited to spiders, or do you also feel anxious around other animals or insects?\n\nPatient: It’s really spiders. I mean, I don’t love every insect, of course, and I might swat a fly like anyone else, but this particular reaction—the sort of… full-body alarm feeling—is tied to spiders.\n\nWith a spider, even if I’m just imagining it, I can feel myself tense up. Like I know perfectly well that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me, and I can say that quite calmly right now. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer in the basement, it suddenly feels huge and threatening in my head. Not huge like it’s actually enormous, but like it takes up all the space in the room somehow. And then I feel silly and embarrassed because I know my reaction doesn’t match the actual danger.\n\nDoctor: Has avoiding spiders and the places where you might see them significantly interfered with your daily life or caused you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I’d like to admit. It’s not that I’m completely unable to live in the house, but owning it has become harder than it should be. I avoid certain parts of the property if I think there might be spiders, and then things just… pile up. Little chores get left longer than they should.\n\nAnd then I end up paying people to do small maintenance things that I probably ought to be able to manage myself, or at least check on myself. It gets expensive, and it makes me feel a bit foolish, honestly, because these aren’t huge jobs. They’re just things I put off because I don’t want to go into the space where I might see one.\n\nSo yes, it causes distress, but it’s also practical. The house is more difficult to keep up with because I’m avoiding areas I actually need to use.\n\nDoctor: Do you also fear other animals or things that resemble spiders, like insects with many legs?\n\nPatient: Not really in the same way, no. I mean, I can be a bit put off by creepy-crawly things generally, but it doesn’t take over like spiders do.\n\nWith spiders, it’s very specific. I can know, perfectly well, that a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that now and believe it, really. But if I picture one dropping out of a box, or being near the washer when I’m doing laundry, it suddenly feels… enormous, threatening, like it has this awful importance in the room. And I feel embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. Other insects might make me uncomfortable, but they don’t have that same effect on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you fear anything that has a similar appearance or movement to spiders, such as ticks or crabs?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s really crabs or ticks specifically, no. I mean, if something moves quickly or has lots of legs, I might get that little shiver of disgust, but it’s not the same thing.\n\nWith spiders, it’s much more… loaded, somehow. I can be completely rational sitting here and say, “A spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me.” I know that. But then the moment I picture one dropping out of a box, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, it feels enormous and threatening in my mind. Not literally enormous, obviously, but it takes over my attention in this ridiculous way.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed to admit that, because I know the reaction doesn’t match the actual danger. But no, I wouldn’t say ticks or crabs bring up that same response for me.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else about spiders that triggers your fear, or is it specifically the spider itself?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the spider itself, I think… or maybe the idea of it suddenly being there, if that makes sense. It’s not so much webs or pictures or anything abstract like that. It’s when I imagine an actual spider in the space with me.\n\nLike, I can sit here and say quite calmly that a spider tucked up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I picture one dropping out of a box I’ve just opened, or sitting near the washer when I’m trying to do laundry, my reaction is completely different. It feels enormous somehow, not physically enormous, but threatening, like it suddenly dominates the whole room and I can’t think properly around it.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s just… the spider being there, close enough that I might have to deal with it, that’s what really sets it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear triggered by the thought of being in a situation where you might encounter a spider, even if you don't actually see one?\n\nPatient: Yes, it can be. It’s not just seeing one, it’s the thought that one might suddenly be there. Like if I’m opening an old box or going into a corner of the house where I haven’t looked for a while, or doing laundry near the washer, I start picturing one appearing.\n\nAnd I know, rationally, a spider sitting in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box, or being right there near the washer, it feels much bigger in my mind than it really is — almost like it becomes the most threatening thing in the room. I’m embarrassed even saying it because I can hear how exaggerated it sounds, but yes, just imagining being in that situation can set it off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders ever triggered by something that reminds you of a spider, but isn't actually a spider?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’ve really noticed that, to be honest. I mean, I suppose there are things that are generally unpleasant if they look crawly or move suddenly, but I don’t think I’ve had the same reaction to something just because it reminds me of a spider.\n\nIt tends to be more… if I think there might actually be one there. Like the possibility of a real spider being nearby. But an object or something that only sort of resembles one — I can’t think of a clear example where that’s set me off in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders only triggered by the actual presence or appearance of a spider, or can it also be triggered by other factors?\n\nPatient: It isn’t only the actual spider in front of me, no. Seeing one is the worst, obviously, but it can start before that, just from the situation or the possibility of one being there.\n\nLike I’ve said, I can be perfectly sensible in my head and know that a spider up in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to do anything to me. I’m not confused about that. But if I’m opening an old box and suddenly picture one dropping out, or if I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to reach, then it changes completely. In my mind it feels enormous, not necessarily in size but in threat, if that makes sense. It’s as if the whole room becomes about that spider and whether it might move or come near me.\n\nI do feel embarrassed saying it out loud, because I know it sounds much bigger than it should be. But yes, it can be triggered by the thought of an actual spider being there, not just by seeing one.\n\nDoctor: Is it something about the way spiders look that triggers your fear, or is it more about what they are or what they might do?\n\nPatient: It’s partly how they look, I suppose — the legs and the quick movement, that does get to me — but honestly I think it’s more about what I imagine they might do, or the fact that they’re there and unpredictable.\n\nI can tell myself, quite clearly, that a spider in the corner of an old house probably isn’t going to hurt me. I do know that. But then if I’m opening a box and I picture one dropping out, or I’m near the washer and imagine one sitting there where I have to put my hand, it suddenly feels enormous and threatening. Not enormous like I think it’s literally huge, but enormous in my mind, like it takes over the whole situation.\n\nAnd I’m embarrassed saying that, because I know it sounds out of proportion. It’s not as if I genuinely believe most spiders are dangerous. But in that moment, it feels like it might move toward me or appear suddenly, and that’s what really sets me off.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders related to how they look or is it something else about them that scares you?\n\nPatient: It’s not just how they look, though the look of them doesn’t help — the legs, the way they can stay still and then suddenly move. But I think the bigger thing is what my mind does with them.\n\nI know, rationally, that if there’s a spider in the corner of an old house, it probably isn’t going to hurt me. But the moment I imagine one dropping out of a box I’m opening, or sitting near the washer where I have to reach in or put my hand near it, it suddenly feels… huge. It’s more the possibility of it suddenly being there, moving, coming near me — that’s what scares me more than just its appearance.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of spiders affected your relationships with friends, family, or colleagues in any significant way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s affected them in a really significant way, no. Not in the sense of, you know, falling out with people or not being able to work with colleagues or anything like that.\n\nI think people close to me know I’m not good with spiders, and they might tease me a bit or be the one to deal with it if one appears, but I haven’t noticed it causing major problems in my relationships. But I can’t think of any serious impact on friends or family, no.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of spiders more about the spider itself or about what might happen if you encounter one?\n\nPatient: It’s more about what might happen, I think.
